*On Tuesday Evening, and on Wednesday at "God is the Lord":*
O Virgin all-immaculate, Mother of Christ God, a sword pierced through thy most holy soul when thou didst behold Thy Son and God voluntarily crucified; Whom, O blessed one, cease not to entreat to grant us forgiveness of transgressions.

*At the end of Matins:*
Thee do we magnify, O Birthgiver of God, crying: Thou art the mountain from which the stone was cut without hands[^533], and it shattered the gates of hades.

*On Wednesday Evening, and on Thursday at "God is the Lord":*
The Word of the Father, Christ our God, Who was incarnate of thee, have we known, O Birthgiver of God, Virgin, sole pure one, sole blessed one: therefore unceasingly hymning thee do we magnify thee.

*At the end of Matins:*
As thou art higher than all creation: *(see fol. 229v)*

*On Thursday Evening, and on Friday at "God is the Lord":*
O Virgin all-immaculate, Mother: *(see fol. 229v)*

*At the end of Matins:*
Thee do we magnify, O Birthgiver of God, crying: Thou art the mountain: *(see fol. 229v)*

*On Friday Evening, and on Saturday at "God is the Lord":*
The mystery hidden from all ages and unknown to Angels was through thee, O Birthgiver of God, made manifest unto those upon earth: God becoming incarnate in unconfused union, and willingly accepting the Cross for our sake, whereby having resurrected the first-formed having resurrected the first-formed, He saved our souls from death.

*At the end of Matins:*
Thee do we magnify, O Birthgiver of God, crying: Thou art the bush: *(see fol. 229v)*

*(Decorative divider band)*

*Tone 5.*
*On Sunday Evening, and on Monday at "God is the Lord":*
With the Angels the heavenly things, with men the earthly things, with a voice of rejoicing, O Birthgiver of God, we cry unto thee: Rejoice, gate more spacious than the heavens! Rejoice, sole salvation of the earthborn! Rejoice, pure one full of grace, who didst bear God incarnate!

*At the end of Matins:*
O Christ God, Who didst shine forth unto the world from a Virgin, and through her didst show us to be sons of light: have mercy on us.

*On Monday Evening, and on Tuesday at "God is the Lord":*
Show thy speedy protection, and help, and mercy upon thy servants, and calm, O pure one, the waves of vain thoughts, and raise up my fallen soul, O Birthgiver of God; for I know, I know, O Virgin, that thou canst do whatsoever thou desirest.

*At the end of Matins:*
O all-pure Mother of God, wall of Christians: deliver thy people as is thy wont, who earnestly cry unto thee; oppose shameful and proud thoughts, that we may cry unto thee: Rejoice, Ever-Virgin!

*On Tuesday Evening, and on Wednesday at "God is the Lord":*
By the Cross of thy Son, O woman graced of God, all the deception of idols hath been abolished, and the demonic might hath been trampled down; therefore, as is our duty, we believers ever hymn and bless thee, and confessing thee to be truly the Birthgiver of God, we magnify thee.

*At the end of Matins:*
O Christ God, Who didst shine forth unto the world from a Virgin: *(see fol. 230r)*

*On Wednesday Evening, and on Thursday at "God is the Lord":*
The strange mystery of the Virgin was shown forth as saving unto the world; for from her wast Thou born without seed, and in the flesh Thou wast shown forth without corruption, O Joy of all: Lord, glory to Thee!

*At the end of Matins:*
O all-pure Mother of God: *(see fol. 230r)*

*On Thursday Evening, and on Friday at "God is the Lord":*
By the Cross of thy Son: *(see fol. 230r)*

*At the end of Matins:*
O Christ God, Who didst shine forth unto the world from a Virgin: *(see fol. 230r)*

*On Friday Evening, and on Saturday at "God is the Lord":*
Rejoice, impassable gate of the Lord! Rejoice, wall and protection of those who flee unto thee! Rejoice, storm-free haven and unwedded one, who didst bear in the flesh thy Creator and God: fail not to pray for those who hymn and worship thy childbearing!

*At the end of Matins:*
O Christ God, Who didst shine forth unto the world from a Virgin: *(see fol. 230r)*

*(Decorative divider band)*

*Tone 6.*
*On Sunday Evening, and on Monday at "God is the Lord":*
The beginning of salvation was Gabriel's greeting unto the Virgin; for she heard the "Rejoice!", and did not shrink from the salutation, nor did she doubt as Sarah in the tent, but thus she said: Behold the handmaid of the Lord; be it unto me according to thy word![^534]

*At the end of Matins:*
Thou didst receive the archangelic word, and wast shown to be the cherubic throne, and in thine arms thou didst bear, O Birthgiver of God, the hope of our souls.

*On Monday Evening, and on Tuesday at "God is the Lord":*
O good Birthgiver of God, Virgin, hope of the world: we ask thine only and mighty protection; have tenderheartedness upon a defenseless people; entreat the merciful God, that our souls may be delivered from every threatening, O sole blessed one.

*At the end of Matins:*
No one that flees unto thee goes away from thee put to shame, O most pure Birthgiver of God, Virgin; but he asks grace, and receives a gift according to his profitable petition.

*On Tuesday Evening, and on Wednesday at "God is the Lord":*
O Birthgiver of God, Virgin: entreat Thy Son, Who was voluntarily nailed upon the Cross, and freed the world from deception, Christ our God, to have mercy on our souls.

*At the end of Matins:*
Him Who before the ages was begotten of the Father without mother, the Son and Word of God, in these last times thou didst bear, incarnate of thy pure blood without man, O Birthgiver of God; Whom do thou entreat, that forgiveness of sins may be granted unto us before the end.

*On Wednesday Evening, and on Thursday at "God is the Lord":*
Holy Sovereign Lady, pure Mother of our God, who ineffably didst bear the Creator of all: entreat His goodness ever with the sacred apostles, to deliver us from passions, and to give us forgiveness of sins.

*At the end of Matins:*
Of great gifts wast thou accounted worthy, O pure Virgin Mother of God; for in the flesh thou didst bear One of the Trinity, Christ the Giver of life, unto the salvation of our souls.

*On Thursday Evening, and on Friday at "God is the Lord":*
O Birthgiver of God, Virgin, entreat Thy Son: *(see fol. 230v)*

*At the end of Matins:*
Him Who before the ages was begotten of the Father without mother: *(see fol. 230v)*

*On Friday Evening, and on Saturday at "God is the Lord":*
Gideon foretells the conception, and David proclaims thy childbearing, O Birthgiver of God; for the Word came down as rain upon the fleece into thy womb, and thou didst sprout forth without seed, O holy land, the Salvation of the world, Christ our God, O full of grace![^535]

*At the end of Matins:*
Him Who before the ages was begotten of the Father without mother: *(see fol. 230v)*

*(Decorative divider band)*

*Tone 7.*
*On Sunday Evening, and on Monday at "God is the Lord":*
O undefiled Virgin Birthgiver of God: entreat Thy Son together with the heavenly hosts, that forgiveness of transgressions may be granted unto us before the end, who faithfully glorify thee.

*At the end of Matins:*
Thou hast surpassed the heavenly hosts, for thou wast shown forth as a divine temple, O blessed Birthgiver of God, having given birth to Christ the Savior of our souls of our souls.

*On Monday Evening, and on Tuesday at "God is the Lord":*
We offer "Rejoice!" unto thee, O Birthgiver of God; for thou wast shown forth higher than the Angels, having given birth to God.

*At the end of Matins:*
By the prayers of the Birthgiver of God, make peaceful our life, who cry unto Thee: O merciful Lord, glory to Thee!

*On Tuesday Evening, and on Wednesday at "God is the Lord":*
Earnestly entreat Christ our God, Who was crucified for us and abolished the dominion of death, O Virgin Birthgiver of God, that He may save our souls.[^536]

*At the end of Matins:*
Deliver us, O Birthgiver of God, who are held fast by sins; for we believers have no other hope save thee, and God Who was born of thee.

*On Wednesday Evening, and on Thursday at "God is the Lord":*
The fruit of thy womb, O most pure one, is the fulfillment of the prophets and the law; therefore, glorifying thee in knowledge as the Birthgiver of God, we piously magnify thee.

*At the end of Matins:*
The fruit of thy womb, O Bride of God, was shown forth unto men as mediating salvation; therefore, glorifying thee as the Birthgiver of God with understanding and tongue, we believers magnify thee.

*On Thursday Evening, and on Friday at "God is the Lord":*
Earnestly entreat Christ our God, Who was crucified for us: *(see fol. 231r)*

*At the end of Matins:*
Deliver us, O Birthgiver of God: *(see fol. 231r)*

*On Friday Evening, and on Saturday at "God is the Lord":*
As a treasury of our resurrection, lead up from the pit and depth of transgressions those who trust in thee, O all-hymned one; for thou didst save those liable to sin, having given birth to our Salvation; who before childbearing wast a Virgin, and in childbearing a Virgin, and after childbearing remainest still a Virgin.

*At the end of Matins:*
Rejoice, thou who didst contain in thy womb Him Whom the heavens cannot contain! Rejoice, O Virgin, proclamation of the prophets, through whom Emmanuel shone forth! Rejoice, Mother of Christ God!

*(Decorative divider band)*

*Tone 8.*
*On Sunday Evening, and on Monday at "God is the Lord":*
Rejoice, thou who through the Angel didst receive the joy of the world! Rejoice, thou who didst bear thy Creator and Lord! Rejoice, thou who wast accounted worthy to be the Mother of God!

*At the end of Matins:*
Rejoice, gate of the King of glory, which the Most High alone passed through, and alone preserved sealed, unto the salvation of our souls.

*On Monday Evening, and on Tuesday at "God is the Lord":*
As the immovable confirmation of faith, and the precious gift of our souls, we believers magnify the Birthgiver of God with hymns: Rejoice, thou who didst contain the Rock of life within thy womb! Rejoice, hope of the ends of the earth, defense of the afflicted! Rejoice, unwedded Bride!

*At the end of Matins:*
O most pure Virgin, save us by thy prayers, moving to compassion the tenderhearted womb of Thy Son and our God.

*On Tuesday Evening, and on Wednesday at "God is the Lord":*
Beholding the Lamb, and Shepherd, and Savior of the world upon the Cross, she that bore Thee said weeping: The world indeed rejoices, receiving deliverance; but my womb burns, beholding Thy crucifixion, which Thou endurest for all, O my Son and my God!

*At the end of Matins:*
The fruit of thy womb, O most pure one, is the fulfillment of the prophets and the law; therefore, glorifying thee in knowledge as the Birthgiver of God, we piously magnify thee.

*On Wednesday Evening, and on Thursday at "God is the Lord":*
O spiritual gate of life, most pure Birthgiver of God: deliver from perils those who faithfully flee unto thee, that we may glorify thy most holy childbearing, unto the salvation of our souls.

*At the end of Matins:*
O most pure Virgin, save us: *(see fol. 231r)*

*On Thursday Evening, and on Friday at "God is the Lord":*
Beholding the Lamb, and Shepherd: *(see fol. 231v)*

*At the end of Matins:*
The fruit of thy womb, O most pure one: *(see fol. 231v)*

*On Friday Evening, and on Saturday at "God is the Lord":*
O Thou Who for our sake wast born of a Virgin, and didst endure crucifixion, O Good One, Who by death didst overthrow death, and didst manifest the Resurrection as God: despise not what Thou hast fashioned with Thy hand; show forth Thy love for mankind, O Merciful One; accept the Birthgiver of God who bore Thee, who prays for us, and save, O our Savior, a desperate people![^537]

*At the end of Matins:*
O most pure Virgin, save us by thy prayers, moving to compassion the tenderhearted womb of Thy Son and our God.

*The End of the Theotokia.*

*(Woodcut armorial tailpiece) (Binding — Rear cover board: Dark leather binding over wooden boards) (Book block — Fore-edge: Red-stained page edges of the codex) (Binding — Rear paste-down: Grey archival endpaper) (Flyleaf — Recto: Grey archival endpaper sheet) (Flyleaf — Verso: Grey archival endpaper sheet) (Flyleaf — Unnumbered blank paper leaf) (Binding — Front cover board: Dark leather binding over wooden boards with blind-stamped central cross, corner pieces, and ornamental roll borders; fragment of paper library label at upper left spine edge) (Binding — Front paste-down: Grey archival endpaper over wooden board with cloth reinforcement hinge along inner joint) (Flyleaf — Recto: Grey archival endpaper sheet with faint pencil inscription at upper margin) (Flyleaf — Verso: Grey archival endpaper sheet) (Flyleaf — Marbled paper endpaper with stone-marbled pattern in black, green, and ochre) (Flyleaf — Recto: Archival paper leaf with handwritten canonical visitation record)*
Presented during the canonical visitation.[^538]
Lviv, 18 March 1934.
+ Andrey, M[etropolitan] *(Book block — Fore-edge: Red-stained page edges of the codex) (Binding — Spine: Dark leather spine with five raised bands and archival library label at foot)*
*Menaion*
*Pochaiv 1761*
*February 3*[^539]
*Old-Print 171/3 (Inv. 353) (Book block — Head edge: Red-stained page edges with sewn endband and leather binding turn-ins)*

### Notes

[^5]: In the physical print of the 1761 Pochaiv Menaion, the podoben heading for the Tone 1 Forefeast stichera is given as *Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ* ("As one valiant among martyrs"), while the stichera of St. Tryphon below are headed Tone 4, *Всехва́льнїи* ("O all-lauded martyrs"). In standard Byzantine and Slavonic liturgical typika, *Всехва́льнїи мꙋ́ченицы* (*Πανεύφημοι μάρτυρες*) is the standard Tone 1 automelon, and *Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ* (*Ὡς γενναῖον ἐν μάρτυσιν*) is the standard Tone 4 automelon. The Pochaiv edition physically inverts the two rubrical model labels.
[^6]: See footnote 5 above regarding the inverted assignment of the Tone 4 model *Всехва́льнїи* to the stichera of St. Tryphon.
[^7]: The attribution to Cyprian (*Купрїа́ново*) indicates authorship by Saint Cyprian (either Cyprian the Hymnographer or Patriarch Cyprian).
[^8]: Saint Germanus I, Patriarch of Constantinople (c. 634–733/740), prolific Byzantine hymnographer.
[^9]: LXX Luke 2:29.
[^10]: LXX Luke 2:32.
[^11]: In the Pochaiv print, the rubric reads *Сщ҃еннагѡ Сѵмеѡ́на* ("Of Holy Symeon"), denoting either an idiomelon hymn in the voice/persona of Righteous Symeon the God-receiver or attributed to Symeon the Metaphrast.
[^12]: Podoben model *Свирѣ́лей па́стырскихъ* (Greek: *Τῶν ποιμενικῶν αὐλῶν*), Tone 8 sessional model from the Nativity / Forefeast cycle.
[^13]: LXX Exodus 14:21–22; 17:11–12.
[^14]: The Church Slavonic acrostic (*краегране́сїе*) reads: *Сла́дости причасти́тисѧ сподо́би мѧ̀ Трѵ́фѡне* ("Account me worthy to partake of sweetness, O Tryphon"), corresponding to the Greek acrostic of the canon by Theophanes (*Γλυκυτάτης μέθεξιν ἀξίωσόν με Τρύφων*).
[^15]: LXX Exodus 14:22, 29.
[^16]: The Katavasia of the Meeting of the Lord: *Сꙋ́шꙋ глꙋбороди́тельнꙋю зе́млю* ("The sun once shone upon dry land / The deep-producing earth").
[^17]: LXX Habakkuk 3:11.
[^18]: LXX Habakkuk 3:2.
[^19]: John 12:46.
[^20]: LXX Isaiah 26:9, 13.
[^21]: LXX Jonah 2:10; John 19:34.
[^22]: LXX Psalm 87:2–3; Jonah 2:3.
[^23]: Daniel 3:52–53 (LXX Song of the Three Holy Youths).
[^24]: Daniel 3:28ff.
[^25]: Daniel 6:22; Daniel 3:57.
[^26]: Daniel 3:19ff.
[^27]: LXX Daniel 2:34, 45. The "cornerstone cut out without hands from an uncut mountain" is a classical Messianic typology of the Virgin Birth.
[^28]: LXX Luke 2:29 (the Canticle of Symeon / *Nunc Dimittis*).
[^29]: LXX Luke 2:34.
[^30]: LXX Luke 2:29–32.
[^31]: LXX Psalm 63:11, 2.
[^32]: LXX Psalm 91:13.
[^33]: LXX Psalm 111:6b–7a.
[^34]: The marginal rubric *Зрѝ* ("Note well" / *Nota bene*) is accompanied by a manicula (pointing hand) in red cinnabar ink.
[^35]: The Katavasia of the Meeting of the Lord: *Сꙋ́шꙋ глꙋбороди́тельнꙋю зе́млю* ("The sun once shone upon dry land / The deep-producing earth").
[^36]: In the Byzantine and Ruthenian liturgical calendar, the preparatory Sundays of the Triodion (Publican and Pharisee, Prodigal Son, Meatfare) can occasionally coincide with the Forefeast or Feast of the Meeting of the Lord depending on the date of Pascha.
[^37]: The marginal rubric *Зрѝ* ("Note well") is printed in red cinnabar with a manicula (pointing hand) in the left margin.
[^38]: Marginal rubric *Зрѝ* in red cinnabar.
[^39]: Marginal rubric *Зрѝ* in red cinnabar.
[^40]: Marginal rubric *Зрѝ* in red cinnabar. The Saturday of Meatfare is the universal ancestral Memorial Saturday (*Вселенская родительская суббота*) dedicated to the commemoration of all departed Christians.
[^41]: Wednesday and Friday of Cheesefare Week are semi-penitential, characterized by Great Compline, prostrations, and the omission of the Divine Liturgy.
[^42]: The Saturday of Cheesefare commemorates all the holy ascetic fathers and mothers who shone forth in the monastic life (*Сыропустная суббота всех преподобных отцов в подвиге просиявших*).
[^43]: LXX Psalm 129:7 (Psalm 130:7).
[^44]: Marginal rubric *Зрѝ* in red cinnabar.
[^45]: Saint Andrew of Crete (c. 650–740), Archbishop of Gortyna in Crete, noted hymnographer and author of the Great Canon.
[^46]: LXX Luke 2:29–30.
[^47]: LXX Luke 2:29.
[^48]: LXX Isaiah 6:6–7. In patristic and Byzantine hymnography, the live coal (*anthrax*) taken by the Seraph with tongs from the altar prefigures Christ borne in the arms of the Virgin Mother of God.
[^49]: LXX Luke 2:32.
[^50]: Saint Germanus I, Patriarch of Constantinople (c. 634–733/740).
[^51]: Saint John of Damascus (c. 676–749), referred to in Byzantine and Slavonic liturgical rubrics as "John the Monk" (*І҆ѡа́ннъ мона́хъ*).
[^52]: LXX Luke 2:29–30.
[^53]: The first Paroemia (Old Testament festal reading) at Great Vespers of the Meeting of the Lord is a liturgical composite cento drawn from Exodus 12:51, 13:1–3, 11–12, 14–16, 22:29; Leviticus 12:2–8; and Numbers 8:16–17, establishing the statutory Law of the firstborn male child and the prescribed purification offerings fulfilled by Christ and the Theotokos.
[^54]: LXX composite festal reading (Exodus 13 / Numbers 8).
[^55]: LXX Isaiah 6:1–12. The second festal Paroemia at Great Vespers of the Meeting of the Lord, recounting the theophany of the Lord of Sabaoth, the Trisagion hymn of the Seraphim, and the Live Coal touching the prophet's lips, prefiguring the incarnation.
[^56]: LXX Isaiah 19:1–5, 12, 16, 19–21. The third festal Paroemia at Great Vespers of the Meeting of the Lord. In the physical Pochaiv 1761 exemplar, the lower margin of Fol. 10r has suffered paper loss, mended with an archival paper patch containing a 19th-century manuscript supply in cursive Cyrillic of verses 4b–5 and 12a.
[^57]: Anatolius of Constantinople (or Anatolius the Studite), 9th-century Byzantine hymnographer.
[^58]: Saint John of Damascus (c. 676–749), referred to in Byzantine and Slavonic liturgical rubrics as "John the Monk" (*І҆ѡа́ннъ мона́хъ*).
[^59]: Saint Andrew Pyrros (or Andrew of Jerusalem / Crete), 7th–8th century hymnographer.
[^60]: Saint Germanus I, Patriarch of Constantinople (c. 634–733/740).
[^61]: Saint Andrew of Crete (c. 650–740).
[^62]: Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (c. 675–752), author of the festal canon of the Meeting of the Lord.
[^63]: LXX Luke 2:29.
[^64]: LXX Luke 2:32.
[^65]: Saint John of Damascus (c. 676–749).
[^66]: Rubric for Great Vespers of the Meeting of the Lord: when an All-Night Vigil (*Бдение*) is celebrated with Litiya, the festal troparion (*Радуйся, благодатная Богородице Дево...*) is sung thrice at the Artoklasia (Blessing of the Loaves).
[^67]: Special festal rubric for Small Compline and Midnight Office on the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord, regulating festal troparia substitutions and omitting penitential prayers.
[^68]: LXX Psalm 44:18a, 2a.
[^69]: Luke 2:25–32 (Matins Gospel for the Meeting of the Lord, narrative of righteous Symeon).
[^70]: LXX Luke 2:29–30.
[^71]: The festal canon of the Meeting of the Lord by Saint Cosmas of Maiuma (*Косма Маюмский*), bearing the alphabetic acrostic in Greek and Slavonic.
[^72]: Ode 1 Heirmos: prefiguring the passage of the Red Sea (Exodus 14–15).
[^73]: Ode 3 Heirmos: based on 1 Kingdoms 2:2 (1 Samuel 2:2) and Acts 20:28.
[^74]: LXX Exodus 33:21–23; 3 Kingdoms 19:11–12; Luke 2:29.
[^75]: Ode 4 Heirmos: LXX Habakkuk 3:3.
[^76]: Ode 5 Heirmos: LXX Isaiah 6:1–5.
[^77]: LXX Isaiah 6:6–7.
[^78]: Ode 6 Heirmos: LXX Luke 2:29–30.
[^79]: LXX Isaiah 8:14; 28:16; Romans 9:33; 1 Peter 2:6–8.
[^80]: LXX Hebrews 1:3.
[^81]: The festal Kontakion of the Meeting of the Lord (*Отробу девичу освятивый...*) attributed to Saint Romanos the Melodist. In this 1761 Pochaiv edition printed under the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth, the phrase *оукрѣпи люди* ("strengthen the people") or *воинство* appears with the word rubbed or replaced, reflecting local political accommodation.
[^82]: Ode 7 Heirmos: Daniel 3:26, 49–50.
[^83]: LXX Luke 2:35.
[^84]: Ode 8 Heirmos: Daniel 3:57–88 (Canticle of the Three Youths).
[^85]: LXX Luke 2:34.
[^86]: The 14 festal refrains (*Припѣвы*) of Ode 9 of the Meeting of the Lord, sung before each Troparion of Ode 9.
[^87]: The 8th festal refrain of Ode 9: *коро́лю на́шемꙋ да́рꙋй* ("grant unto our King"), explicitly naming the King of Poland (Augustus III of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth, r. 1734–1763) in whose realm the Pochaiv Lavra was situated in 1761.
[^88]: Rubric governing the performance of the 14 refrains with the Heirmos and Troparia of Ode 9 at Matins.
[^89]: Ode 9 Heirmos: Exodus 13:2, Luke 2:23.
[^90]: LXX Psalm 50:14 (Psalm 51:12); Luke 2:29.
[^91]: Festal Exapostilarion (*Светилен*) of the Meeting of the Lord: LXX Luke 2:29–32.
[^92]: Saint Germanus I, Patriarch of Constantinople (c. 634–733/740).
[^93]: Rubric for the conclusion of Festal Matins and the First Hour.
[^94]: Rubric for the festal outdoor stauropegial candlelit cross procession (*крестный ход / лития вне обители*) at the second hour of the day (approx. 8:00 AM) traditionally observed at the Pochaiv Lavra on the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord (Candlemas), followed by the Little Hours and the Festal Divine Liturgy.
[^95]: The Festal Introit (*Входный стих*) at the Divine Liturgy of the Meeting of the Lord: LXX Psalm 97:2.
[^96]: Festal Prokeimenon of the Theotokos: LXX Luke 1:46–48 (Canticle of the Theotokos / *Magnificat*).
[^97]: Epistle to the Hebrews, Pericope 316 (Hebrews 7:7–17), appointed as the festal Epistle for the Divine Liturgy of the Meeting of the Lord.
[^98]: Gospel according to Luke, Pericope 7 (Luke 2:22–40), the festal Gospel for the Divine Liturgy of the Meeting of the Lord.
[^99]: LXX Psalm 115:4 (116:13 Masoretic). Festal Communion verse (*Koinonikon*) for the Meeting of the Lord.
[^100]: Rubrical Mark Chapter (*Маркова глава*) regulating the concurrence (*согласование*) of the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord (February 2) when it occurs on a pre-Lenten Sunday prior to the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee.
[^101]: Rubric regulating the concurrence of the Meeting of the Lord with the four pre-Lenten Sundays of the Triodion: Publican and Pharisee, Prodigal Son, Meatfare, or Cheesefare.
[^102]: Triodion Litiya stichera transferred from the Praises of Sunday Matins to Saturday Great Vespers to ensure that no stichera of the Triodion remain omitted.
[^103]: LXX Psalm 136:1 ("By the rivers of Babylon"), the third Polyeleos psalm chanted on the three Sundays preceding Great Lent (Prodigal Son, Meatfare, Cheesefare).
[^104]: The Slavonic text on Fol. 16r–v exhibits an apparent typographical haplography or dialectal typesetting in the incipit of the Meatfare Praises Doxasticon: Fol. 16r ends with `Пра́-` and catchword `шꙋчи́тимъ`, and Fol. 16v begins `шꙋчи́стимъ себѐ бра́тїе` (*Прашꙋчистимъ / Праочистимъ себе, братие*), elsewhere correctly printed on Fol. 19r as `Предѡчи́стимъ себѐ` (*Let us cleanse ourselves beforehand, brethren*).
[^105]: Rubric governing the celebration of the Meeting of the Lord when falling on Meatfare Saturday (*Суббота мясопустная*), the Universal Ancestral Memorial Day (*Вселенская родительская суббота*). The commemoration of the reposed is anticipated on the preceding Saturday or transferred to Meatfare Thursday, leaving Meatfare Saturday exclusively to the feast.
[^106]: Rubric for the Meeting of the Lord occurring on Cheesefare Monday, Tuesday, or Thursday (*Сырная седмица*).
[^107]: Rubric for the Meeting of the Lord occurring on Cheesefare Wednesday or Friday, traditionally non-Eucharistic fast days. The Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom is celebrated, with three great prostrations (*великие поклоны*) at the conclusion of the offices.
[^108]: LXX Psalm 130:7 (Psalm 129:7 Masoretic).
[^109]: Commemoration of All the Holy Monastic and Ascetic Fathers who have shone forth in the Ascetic Life (*Память всех преподобных отцов в подвиге просиявших*), celebrated annually on Cheesefare Saturday.
[^110]: LXX Psalm 115:6 (Psalm 116:15 Masoretic). Prokeimenon verse for the venerable monastic fathers.
[^111]: Rubric for the Meeting of the Lord falling on Clean Monday (*Чистый понедельник*), the opening day of Great Lent. In order to preserve the strict penance of the First Week, the festal celebration is anticipated and sung on Cheesefare Sunday (*Прощеное воскресенье*).
[^112]: Rubric governing the concurrence of the Afterfeast (*попразднство*) of the Meeting of the Lord with a pre-Lenten Sunday prior to the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee.
[^113]: Rubric for the concurrence of the Afterfeast of the Meeting of the Lord with the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee, Prodigal Son, or Meatfare.
[^114]: Rubric for the Afterfeast of the Meeting falling on Meatfare Saturday: the celebration of the Afterfeast is entirely omitted (*оставляется*), and the universal commemoration of the faithful departed (*память всех от века усопших*) is celebrated without diminution.
[^115]: Rubric for the Afterfeast of the Meeting falling on Monday, Tuesday, or Thursday of Cheesefare Week (*Сырная седмица*).
[^116]: Rubric for the Afterfeast of the Meeting falling on Cheesefare Wednesday or Friday: Great Compline is sung starting from "When I called upon Thee" (*Внегда призвати ми*, LXX Psalm 4:2), with three great prostrations (*великие поклоны*) at the close of each office.
[^117]: In the Byzantine-Ruthenian Typikon, Cheesefare Saturday commemorates All Holy Ascetic Fathers (Commemoration of All the Righteous Monks and Ascetics). When the Afterfeast of the Meeting of the Lord coincides with Cheesefare Saturday, the services of the feast and the ascetic Fathers are combined as specified in these rubrics.
[^118]: February 3 commemorates the Holy and Righteous Symeon the God-Receiver and Anna the Prophetess, who met the Christ Child in the Temple on the 40th day after His Nativity (Luke 2:22–38). In the liturgical cycle, this day functions as the Synaxis of the Meeting of the Lord.
[^119]: Attributed to St. Germanus, Patriarch of Constantinople (c. 634–733 or 740), author of numerous festal stichera and liturgical hymns.
[^120]: Attributed to St. John the Monk (St. John of Damascus, c. 676–749).
[^121]: Acrostic in the Theotokia and troparia of the Saint's Canon: "Receive the song, O Symeon the God-Receiver. By Joseph" (St. Joseph the Hymnographer, c. 816–886).
[^122]: The 1761 Pochaiv Menaion reads diplomatically 'and strengthen the king whom Thou hast loved' (*и оукрѣпи короля егоже возлюбилъ*), reflecting the political sovereignty of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth under King Augustus III of Poland (reigned 1734–1763), where the Pochaiv Basilian press operated, unlike Russian Synodal editions which read *христоименитых людей* or *императора*.

[^282]: Physical leaf p91 bears the printed foliation Fol. 45r (`м҃е`), beneath the running head for the 7th Day of February (`з҃ ДЕНЬ`).
[^281]: Recensional variant: The Pochaiv Menaion includes a petition for the reigning King (*"и҆ оу҆крѣпѝ королѧ̀"*, "and strengthen the King") in the festal Kontakion of the Meeting of the Lord, reflecting Ruthenian Greek Catholic usage under the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth (King Augustus III, r. 1734–1763), whereas contemporary Muscovite Synodal editions read *"и҆ оу҆крѣпѝ правосла́вныхъ хрїстїа́нъ"* ("and strengthen Orthodox Christians") or *"лю́ди твоѧ̑"* ("Thy people").
[^283]: The Slavonic phrase *"ѿ всѧ́кагѡ вре́да ры́бна"* refers to St. Parthenios of Lampsakos, who supported the poor by his labor as a fisherman and miraculously protected fishermen, their boats, and their catches from demonic attacks and storms.
[^284]: Physical leaf p92 represents Fol. 45v, beneath the running head *"Month of February"*.
[^285]: Physical leaf p93 represents Fol. 46r (`м҃ѕ`), with quire signature *Кв*.
[^286]: Physical leaf p94 represents Fol. 46v, beneath the running head *"Month of February"*.
[^287]: Physical leaf p95 bears the printed foliation Fol. 47r (`м҃з`), beneath the running head for the 7th Day of February (`з҃ ДЕНЬ`).
[^288]: Physical leaf p96 represents Fol. 47v, marking the commemoration of the Holy Great-Martyr Theodore the Stratelates (General) and the Holy Prophet Zechariah (the Sickle-Seer) on the 8th Day of February.
[^289]: The name *Theodore* (*Θεόδωρος*, Church Slavonic *Ѳео́дѡръ*) signifies "Gift of God" (*Божий дар*), an etymological play highlighted in the incipit of this Doxastichon (*"Бж҃їихъ даро́въ ꙗ҆́кѡ тезоимени́та"*, "As one whose name bespeaks the gifts of God"). Physical leaf p97 bears foliation Fol. 48r (`м҃и`).
[^290]: Physical leaf p98 represents Fol. 48v, beneath the running head *"Month of February"*.
[^291]: Physical leaf p99 represents Fol. 49r (`м҃ѳ`), bearing quire signature *Л*.
[^292]: Physical leaf p100 represents Fol. 49v, concluding Cohort 10 with the third Ode of the Canons for the Afterfeast and February 8 commemorations.
[^293]: The Pochaiv edition reads королѧ̀ (*the King*, referring to the reigning monarch Augustus III of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth), where contemporary Russian Synodal editions read лю́ди (*the people*) or царѧ̀ (*the Tsar*).
[^294]: The rubrics cross-reference the common Sessional Theotokia and Cross-Theotokia for Lenten weekdays (or fast periods) provided at the conclusion of the month under February 28 (fol. 106v).
[^295]: The printed text reads Дѣво́мъ (or Дево́мъ), an evident typographical error either for Дѣ́вою (*Through the Virgin*), or for Дрѣ́вомъ / Дре́вомъ (*By the Tree*), the latter being a common patristic motif of Christ renewing mankind through the Wood of the Cross in reversal of Adam's fall.
[^296]: Pericope 292 of the Apostle is 2 Timothy 2:1–10, the appointed epistle lection for a martyr (*"Thou therefore, my son, be strong in the grace that is in Christ Jesus..."*).
[^297]: Pericope 36 of the Gospel according to Matthew is Matthew 10:16–22, the appointed gospel lection for a martyr (*"Behold, I send you forth as sheep in the midst of wolves..."*).
[^298]: The printed heading reads *гла́съ, н҃*, an apparent typographical error for *гла́съ, и҃* (Tone 8), as confirmed by the standard Tone 8 Heirmologion heirmoi (*Колесницегонителѧ фараѡ́на*, etc.) utilized throughout this canon composed by St. Theophanes Graptos.
[^299]: The printed Slavonic text reads *Оу҆́мъ нѐныи*, an evident typographical error either for *Оу҆́мъ нб҃ный* (*The heavenly mind*) or *зе́мный* (*The earthly mind*), expressing the traditional patristic theological axiom that no created intellect can comprehend the mystery of the Incarnation.
[^300]: The printed Church Slavonic text reads *Стѧ́гши*, an obscure form or scribal error perhaps for *Стѧжа́въ* (*Having acquired...*) or *Стѧ́гомъ* (*Under the banner of Christ's martyrs...*).
[^301]: The printed text has *промышле́нїю, ве́мъ:* across the column break, dropping the syllable *зо-* from *зове́мъ* (*we cry*).
[^302]: The printed text reads *Ѿсщїе́тїѧ*, an evident typographical corruption for *Ѿсѣче́нїемъ* (*By his beheading* / *By cutting off*), translating the Greek hymnographic term *Τῇ τομῇ*.
[^303]: The printed Slavonic text reads *Н҆́бо ѕвѣзда́ми:* with an initial *Н* bearing a smooth breathing/titlo, representing *Не́бо ѕвѣзда́ми* (the standard Tone 3 Exapostilarion model, Greek *Ὁ οὐρανὸν τοῖς ἄστροις*).
[^304]: The rubric notes that the commemorated daily saint whose service is superseded by the Apodosis of the Meeting of the Lord (in this case, St. Nikephoros) is transferred to Compline.
[^305]: The printed abbreviation *въ вл҃к:* represents *въ воскресе́нїе* or *въ недѣ́лю госпо́дскую / влады́чню* (*on the Lord's Day / on Sunday*).
[^306]: That is, the Commemoration of All the Holy Ascetic Fathers who shone forth in the Ascetic Life, celebrated on Cheesefare Saturday.
[^307]: The printed heading reads *Ст҃а́гѡ мч҃нка Харала́мпїа* (*Holy Martyr Charalampes*). In liturgical tradition and subsequent hymnography within this same office (e.g., Ode 1 of the canon), he is also commemorated as a Hieromartyr (bishop of Magnesia in Thessaly).
[^308]: The printed Church Slavonic text reads *свѧще́нниче плѣ́не*, an unusual vocative form perhaps rendering Greek *ἱερὲ πρεσβῦτα* (*sacred elder*) misread or corrupted in transmission, or signifying *sacred captive* / *chosen one*.
[^309]: St. Charalampes is invoked as 'namesake of joy' because his name in Greek, Charalampos (Χαράλαμπος), derives from *chará* (χαρά, 'joy') and *lámpein* (λάμπειν, 'to shine').
[^310]: The printed Church Slavonic text reads *сꙋ́мное*, an apparent graphical error or dialectal form for *шꙋ́мное* (*surging* / *tempestuous*), translating Greek *κυμαινόμενον* or *πολυτάραχον*.
[^311]: An allusion to the traditional hagiographical account of St. Charalampes's passion, wherein an irrational beast was granted speech by divine power to confound the persecutor Lucian.
[^312]: St. Blaise (Vlasij), Bishop of Sebaste in Armenia, martyred under Licinius c. 316.
[^313]: In the passion of St. Blaise, seven devout women followed him during his trial, collected the drops of his blood, and were themselves martyred after refusing to sacrifice to idols.
[^314]: An etymological wordplay on the name Blaise (*Vlasij*), associated in Byzantine hymnography with *blastánein* (βλαστάνειν, 'to sprout, shoot forth, germinate', Slavonic *прозѧбати*).
[^315]: Alluding to the tradition that beasts of the wilderness gathered around St. Blaise in his cave on Mount Argeus, where he healed them of their infirmities.
[^316]: Referring both to LXX Ps. 76:20 ("Thy way is in the sea, and Thy paths in many waters, and Thy footsteps shall not be known") and to the miracle in St. Blaise's passion where he walked across the surface of a lake as on dry land, challenging his persecutors to do the same.
[^317]: In the passion of the seven women who suffered with St. Blaise, when their flesh was torn with iron combs, milk flowed from their bodies instead of blood.
[^318]: In his final prayer before beheading, St. Blaise petitioned that whoever honored his memory should receive healing from throat diseases and protection for their households and livestock.
[^319]: St. Meletius, Archbishop of Antioch (reigned 360–381), staunch defender of Nicene orthodoxy against Arianism and presiding hierarch of the Second Ecumenical Council (Constantinople I, 381), where he reposed during the conciliar sessions.
[^320]: Greek acrostic: *Τὰς σὰς ἐπαίνους ἐκφαίνω, μάκαρ* ("Thy praises I declare, O blessed one"), attributed in Byzantine menaia to St. Theophanes the Branded.
[^321]: Referring to St. Meletius's spiritual adoption through grace, in contrast to Christ Who is Son by nature and essence, a foundational distinction against the Arians.
[^322]: In his famous Antiochian sermons, St. Meletius demonstrated the Holy Trinity by holding up three fingers and then bringing them together as one, thereby avoiding both the tritheism/subordinationism of Arius and the modalistic monarchianism of Sabellius.
[^323]: Macedonius I of Constantinople, heresiarch who denied the divinity of the Holy Spirit (Pneumatomachianism / Macedonians), condemned at the Second Ecumenical Council under the presidency of St. Meletius.
[^324]: St. Meletius presided over the Second Ecumenical Council (Constantinople I, 381), which formulated the Niceno-Constantinopolitan Creed's article on the Holy Spirit.
[^325]: The *Pneumatomachians* ('Spirit-fighters') or Macedonians, who denied the full divinity of the Holy Spirit, opposed by St. Meletius.
[^326]: St. Martinian of Caesarea in Palestine (5th c.), ascetic who struggled from youth in the wilderness, resisted the temptation of the courtesan Zoe (whom he converted), and spent years upon an isolated sea rock before wandering until his holy repose in Athens.
[^327]: When the woman Zoe sought to seduce St. Martinian in his hermitage, he rekindled the fire of charcoal and stepped barefoot into the burning embers to vanquish carnal passion with the remembrance of eternal fire.
[^328]: Following his encounter with Zoe, St. Martinian retreated to an uninhabited rocky reef in the sea, dwelling under the open sky without a roof, until he survived a shipwrecked maiden, whom he left on the rock while casting himself into the sea to preserve his chastity, miraculously brought ashore by dolphins.
[^329]: Alluding to the prophecy of the Righteous Simeon in Luke 2:35 ("Yea, a sword shall pierce through thine own soul also"), fulfilled at the foot of the Cross.
[^330]: Referring to Zoe, the woman sent by the adversary to tempt St. Martinian in his desert cell, whom he converted to repentance, directing her to a monastery in Bethlehem where she lived out her days in asceticism.
[^331]: St. Martinian, feeling the temptation of the flesh, stepped barefoot into burning coals, saying to himself: "If thou canst not bear this temporary fire, Martinian, how wilt thou endure the eternal fire of Gehenna?"
[^332]: The Slavonic text reads literally: *И҆сплени́лъ твоѝ гдⷭ҇и во поще́нїи свое́мъ по́двиги показа́въ...* (a Pochaiv print corruption for *Τὰς σὰς ὁσίας ἐν νηστείαις δείξας πράξεις*), addressing Christ concerning the ascetic struggles of St. Martinian.
[^333]: St. Martinian retreated to an isolated sea reef/rock in the Mediterranean, living under the open sky for ten years without any shelter from sun or storm.
[^334]: After saving the shipwrecked maiden Photina and leaving the sea rock to preserve his chastity, St. Martinian spent his remaining years as a homeless wanderer (*strannik*), passing through numerous cities until his repose in Athens.
[^335]: When St. Martinian cast himself into the sea from the rock to flee temptation, two dolphins miraculously bore him upon their backs to the shore.
[^336]: St. Martinian foresaw his approaching death, entered a cathedral church in Athens, lay down upon the floor, and having asked the local bishop to bury his remains, commended his soul to God.
[^337]: Referring to Photina, the maiden shipwrecked upon St. Martinian's rock, whom he left to pursue asceticism there while he plunged into the sea. She lived on the rock for six years in prayer and fasting until her holy repose.
[^338]: St. Auxentius of Bithynia (5th c.), former courtier under Emperor Theodosius the Younger, who renounced worldly rank to become a solitary ascetic on Mount Oxia and Mount Sinope (later called Mount of Auxentius). He played an influential role at the Fourth Ecumenical Council (Chalcedon, 451) in defending the Orthodox dogma against Monophysitism.
[^339]: The Greek acrostic reads: *Αὐξέντιον τὸν θεῖον ᾄδω σοι, μάκαρ* ("Unto thee, O blessed one, I sing of divine Auxentius"), composed by St. Theophanes the Branded (c. 778–845).
[^340]: Referring to the Orthodox dogma affirmed at the Fourth Ecumenical Council of Chalcedon (451) in which St. Auxentius participated, confessing Christ in two unconfused natures and Mary as truly *Theotokos*.
[^341]: St. Onesimus of the Seventy Apostles, disciple of the Holy Apostle Paul (commemorated in the Epistle to Philemon), formerly a runaway slave of Philemon in Colossae, who was baptized by Paul in Rome, sent back as a beloved brother, and later served as bishop (traditionally of Ephesus or Byzantium) before suffering martyrdom by stoning in Rome under Emperor Trajan or Domitian.
[^342]: The Slavonic acrostic reads: Ѡ҆ страсте́й мѧ̀ раба̀ сꙋ́ща и҆зба́ви бл҃же́нне. Ѳеофа́нъ ("Deliver me who am a slave to passions, O blessed one. Theophanes").
[^343]: Cf. Philemon 1:10: "I beseech thee for my son Onesimus, whom I have begotten in my bonds."
[^344]: "He who was called from heaven" refers to the holy Apostle Paul, called by Christ on the road to Damascus (Acts 9:3–6).
[^345]: The Church Slavonic phrase "дре́внагѡ ко́жнагѡ ᲂу҆мерщвле́нїѧ" refers to the patristic typology of the garments of skins (Gen. 3:21) signifying mortality and fallen human nature.
[^346]: The Greek name Pamphilus (Πάμφιλος) signifies "beloved of all," corresponding to the Slavonic "прелюбе́зенъ тезоименити́твꙋ" ("beloved according to his name").
[^347]: The acrostic in Church Slavonic reads: Собо́ръ дванадесѧточи́сленный страстоте́рпцевъ воспою̀. Творе́нїе Ѳеофа́ново ("I will hymn the twelve-numbered choir of the passion-bearers. The work of Theophanes").
[^348]: The twelve martyrs correspond in number to the twelve apostles and the twelve minor prophets.
[^349]: The five Egyptian companions of Saint Pamphilus adopted the names of the holy prophets: Samuel, Elias, Daniel, Jeremias, and Isaias.
[^350]: The physical print assigns the podoben "As one valiant among martyrs" (Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ / Ὡς γενναῖον ἐν Μάρτυσιν) to Tone 2, whereas in the standard Byzantine and modern Ruthenian Typikon tradition this model is Tone 4.
[^351]: Theodore the Tyro set fire to the temple of Cybele (the pagan "Mother of the Gods") in Amasea, confessing Christ alone as true God.
[^352]: Cf. Luke 2:35: "Yea, a sword shall pierce through thine own soul also."
[^353]: The name Theodore (Θεόδωρος) signifies "gift of God" (Slavonic: бж҃е́ственныи да́ръ). The hymn plays upon the martyr's name: "both in deed and in name" (и҆ вѣ́щь, и҆ и҆́ма / καὶ πράγματι καὶ ὀνόματι).
[^354]: Theodore the Tyro was martyred by being cast into a furnace in the year 306 during the persecution under Maximian.
[^355]: Cf. 2 Tim. 4:7–8: "I have fought a good fight, I have finished my course, I have kept the faith: Henceforth there is laid up for me a crown of righteousness."
[^356]: St. Theodore was suspended upon a wooden post and scraped with iron claws before being cast into the fire.
[^357]: Cf. Rom. 8:38–39: "For I am persuaded, that neither death, nor life, nor angels... shall be able to separate us from the love of God, which is in Christ Jesus our Lord."
[^358]: The text of the ikos directly addresses Christ, thanking Him for granting the martyr Theodore as an invincible champion.
[^359]: St. Theodore's name is derived from Greek Θεός ("God") and δῶρον ("gift").
[^360]: Christ is mystically called the "Cluster of grapes" (Gk. βότρυς), crushed upon the winepress of the Cross to yield the wine of eternal life.
[^361]: Pope Saint Leo the Great (reigned 440–461) was Bishop of Rome and author of the famous *Tome to Flavian*, which defined the orthodox Christological dogma of the two natures in Christ.
[^362]: The "supreme council" refers to the Fourth Ecumenical Council of Chalcedon (451), where Pope Leo's Tome was enthusiastically received with the acclamation: "Peter has spoken through Leo!"
[^363]: Slavonic: "мѣ́рило и҆мꙋ́ще и҆справле́нїе" (possessing an upright balance or measure of correction).
[^364]: Pope Leo's *Tome to Flavian* refuted both Nestorianism (which divided Christ into two persons) and Eutychian Monophysitism (which confused Christ's divine and human natures into one).
[^365]: This troparion is the common Troparion of a Hierarch and Doctor of the Church (Tone 8), specifically applied here to St. Leo the Great.
[^366]: As Bishop of Rome, St. Leo is celebrated as the direct successor to the Chair of Saint Peter.
[^367]: Slavonic: "сꙋгꙋ́бо ре́къ сꙋщество̀, и҆ сꙋгꙋ́бо дѣ́йство" (declaring two essences/natures and two operations/energies in Christ), directly referencing Leo's *Tome to Flavian* (Epistola 28).
[^368]: An allusion to Proverbs 28:1 ("the righteous are bold as a lion") and a play upon the name Leo (Latin for "lion").
[^369]: Allusion to the Song of Solomon 2:15 ("Take us the foxes, the little foxes, that spoil the vines") interpreted as heretics troubling the Church.
[^370]: St. Leo, being from Rome (the West), sent his *Tome* eastward to the Council of Chalcedon.
[^371]: A literal citation of St. Leo's *Tome*: "Agit enim utraque forma cum alterius communione quod proprium est" ("Each nature performs what is proper to it in communion with the other").
[^372]: Cf. Chalcedonian definition: Christ acknowledged in two natures, inconfusedly, unchangeably, indivisibly, inseparably (ἀσυγχύτως, ἀτρέπτως, ἀδιαιρέτως, ἀχωρίστως).
[^373]: Incarnation without confusion or change of either the divinity or the humanity.
[^374]: The "divinely moved scroll" (бг҃одвижи́мый сви́токъ) is Pope Leo's *Tome to Flavian*.
[^375]: "Noetic lions" (львѡ́въ оу҆ста̑ слове́сныхъ) refers metaphorically to fierce heretical teachers (Nestorius, Eutyches, Dioscorus) whose arguments St. Leo silenced.
[^376]: Nestorius refused to acknowledge the Virgin Mary as *Theotokos* (Birthgiver of God), asserting she bore only the human person of Christ.
[^377]: Cf. Psalm 131:4 (LXX): "If I will give sleep to mine eyes, or slumber to mine eyelids..."
[^378]: Dyothelitism and dyoenergism (two wills and two operations/energies in Christ according to His two natures), defended by Pope Leo in his *Tome*.
[^379]: The Chalcedonian adverbs: ἀδιαιρέτως (нераздѣ́льнѡ / indivisibly), ἀτρέπτως (непревра́тнѡ / unalterably), ἀσυγχύτως (несли́ѧннѡ / inconfusedly).
[^380]: Continuation of the Canon to St. Leo, Pope of Rome, Ode 8. Daniel 3:57.
[^381]: Allusion to Psalm 22:2 (LXX) / Psalm 23:2 (MT): "In a place of green pasture, there hath He made me to dwell."
[^382]: Allusion to Psalm 35:9 (LXX) / Psalm 36:8 (MT): "And Thou shalt make them drink of the torrent of Thy pleasure [sweetness]."
[^383]: St. Archippus, Apostle of the Seventy, companion and fellow laborer of the Apostle Paul (Colossians 4:17; Philemon 1:2).
[^384]: The martyrdom of St. Archippus, who was buried up to his waist in a pit and stoned and pierced by needles by children and the pagan mob during a festival of Artemis at Chonae.
[^385]: Theotokion invoking deliverance from the ancestral enemy, depicted as a predatory man-slaying serpent.
[^386]: Allusion to Habakkuk 1:15 and the apostolic commission to be "fishers of men" (Matthew 4:19).
[^387]: Philemon 1:2: "And to our beloved Apphia, and Archippus our fellow soldier" (Church Slavonic: сво́инъ / сїинъ, Greek: συστρατιώτῃ).
[^388]: Referring to the persecution of Christians under Nero, during which Archippus was apprehended.
[^389]: Allusion to Matthew 5:13 and Colossians 4:6: "Let your speech be always with grace, seasoned with salt."
[^390]: Habakkuk 3:3 (LXX): "God shall come from Teman, and the Holy One from the dark and shady mountain."
[^391]: Allusion to John 7:38: "Out of his belly shall flow rivers of living water."
[^392]: The torture of St. Archippus, who was dragged along the ground by four executioners (or beasts).
[^393]: Christ the Rock / Stone of Life (1 Corinthians 10:4; 1 Peter 2:4).
[^394]: The martyrdom of St. Archippus, who was buried in the ground up to his waist and stoned.
[^395]: Philemon 1:2.
[^396]: St. Apphia, commemorated with St. Philemon and St. Archippus on February 19 and November 22. According to tradition, she was the wife of Philemon or the mother/spiritual kin of Archippus.
[^397]: St. Archippus was bishop or prominent minister of the church at Colossae (Colossians 4:17).
[^398]: St. Leo the Wonderworker, Bishop of Catania in Sicily (c. 709–787), noted for his charity, miracles, and his contest with the sorcerer Heliodorus.
[^399]: The common troparion/sticheron theme honoring the saint as a living pillar of the Church (Galatians 2:9; 1 Timothy 3:15).
[^400]: Allusion to the Magnificat: "For He that is mighty hath magnified me" (Luke 1:49).
[^401]: The common Troparion of a Hierarch ("Rule of faith...").
[^402]: Romans 12:1: "Present your bodies a living sacrifice, holy, acceptable unto God."
[^403]: Reference to St. Leo's elevation to the episcopal see of Catania.
[^404]: St. Leo's relics in Catania poured forth fragrant, healing myrrh.
[^405]: Allusion to Isaiah 6:6; the tongs holding the live coal prefigure the Theotokos bearing Christ without being burned.
[^406]: Matthew 13:8, 23: the good ground yielding fruit a hundredfold.
[^407]: The myrrh-streaming tomb and shrine of St. Leo in Catania.
[^408]: St. Leo's public challenge of the sorcerer Heliodorus, whom he seized by his pallium and led into a blazing furnace; Leo emerged untouched, while Heliodorus was consumed by the fire.
[^409]: Allusion to the Three Youths in the Babylonian furnace (Daniel 3:49–50), where an angel brought down a moist whistling wind / dew.
[^410]: The miraculous healings wrought by St. Leo during his episcopate.
[^411]: St. Leo appearing before the Byzantine Emperor in Constantinople carrying red-hot embers in the fold of his omophorion/felonion without harming his vestment.
[^412]: The miraculous healing oil flowing from the relics of St. Leo.
[^413]: Allusion to the miracles of Christ and the apostles: Isaiah 35:5–6; Matthew 11:5; Acts 3:6–8.
[^414]: Church Slavonic: Наїо́дѡръ (for Їлїо́дѡръ / Heliodorus). Heliodorus was a former Christian who apostatized and practiced sorcery in Catania. St. Leo wrapped his omophorion around him and stood with him in the public bonfire; Heliodorus was incinerated while St. Leo's vestments and flesh were unharmed.
[^415]: Psalm 44:3 (LXX) / Psalm 45:2 (MT): "Grace is poured into thy lips: therefore God hath blessed thee for ever."
[^416]: Recalling the miracle before Emperor Leo IV the Khazar or Constantine V Kopronymos, when Bishop Leo proved the divine favor by carrying burning coals in his garment.
[^417]: Allusion to Psalm 51:10 (LXX) / Psalm 52:8 (MT): "But I am like a fruitful olive tree in the house of the Lord."
[^418]: Theotokion of Ode 8 for St. Leo of Catania: deification (theosis) through the Incarnation of Christ.
[^419]: Invocatory troparion of Ode 9 petitioning St. Leo of Catania for deliverance from passions and afflictions.
[^420]: Allusion to Christ as the True Vine and believers as the branches (John 15:1–5).
[^421]: The Church of the Holy Martyr Lucy (Lucia of Syracuse) in Catania, built by Bishop Leo, where his honorable relics were entombed and worked miracles.
[^422]: Joint commemoration of St. Timothy of Symbola (an 8th-century ascetic on Mount Olympus in Bithynia) and St. Eustathius, Archbishop of Antioch (reigned c. 324–330; one of the leading Nicene fathers of the First Ecumenical Council, 325, who opposed Arianism and was subsequently exiled to Thrace).
[^423]: Allusion to Abraham's hospitality (Genesis 18), Job's endurance in affliction (Job 1:21–22, 2:10), and David's meekness (LXX Psalm 131:1).
[^424]: St. Eustathius's championing of the Nicene confession against Arius, affirming the consubstantiality (homoousios) and equal glory of the Son with the Father.
[^425]: The common troparion of the Holy Ascetic Fathers and Hierarchs in Tone 4 ("Боже отецъ нашихъ...").
[^426]: Exodus 15:1–21: the Song of Moses after the crossing of the Red Sea and the destruction of Pharaoh's host.
[^427]: Allusion to St. Eustathius of Antioch refuting the Arian denial of the co-eternal divinity of Christ.
[^428]: 1 Samuel 2:2 (1 Kings 2:2 LXX): the Song of Hannah ("There is none holy as the Lord: for there is none beside Thee: neither is there any rock like our God").
[^429]: Colossians 3:5: "Mortify therefore your members which are upon the earth; fornication, uncleanness, inordinate affection, evil concupiscence, and covetousness, which is idolatry."
[^430]: 1 Samuel 2:1 (1 Kings 2:1 LXX): "My heart rejoices in the Lord, mine horn is exalted in the Lord: my mouth is enlarged over mine enemies; because I rejoice in Thy salvation."
[^431]: Matthew 13:24–30 (the parable of the wheat and the tares).
[^432]: Allusion to the assembly of the 318 Holy Fathers at the First Ecumenical Council in Nicaea (325).
[^433]: Luke 1:28, the angelic salutation of Archangel Gabriel at the Annunciation.
[^434]: The Stavrotheotokion lament of the Theotokos contrasting the manifold miracles of Christ with His unjust condemnation.
[^435]: Habakkuk 3:2–3 LXX: "O Lord, I have heard Thy report, and was afraid: I considered Thy works, and was amazed... God shall come from Teman, and the Holy One from the dark and shady mountain."
[^436]: LXX Psalm 63:8 (MT Psalm 64:7): "The arrows of infants have become their wounds."
[^437]: St. Timothy of Symbola lived during the Byzantine iconoclast controversy and suffered persecutions for defending the veneration of the holy icons.
[^438]: Habakkuk 3:3 LXX: "His virtue covered the heavens, and the earth was full of His praise."
[^439]: Isaiah 5:1–7: the parable of the vineyard of the Lord of hosts.
[^440]: Allusion to Genesis 1:2–3 and Isaiah 26:9.
[^441]: The hagiographical account of St. Timothy of Symbola, whose prayers granted conception and healthy childbirth to a woman who had long been barren.
[^442]: Isaiah 26:9 LXX: "By night my spirit seeks Thee early in the morning, O God, for Thy commandments are light upon the earth."
[^443]: Jonah 2:2–3, LXX Psalm 129:1: the cry of Jonah from the belly of the whale and the deep of the sea.
[^444]: The ascetic discipline of St. Timothy of Symbola, who took a strict perpetual vow never to look upon the face of any woman.
[^445]: Allusion to the hermit life of St. Timothy in the wilderness caves and peaks of Mount Olympus in Bithynia.
[^446]: Jonah 2:2: "Out of the belly of hades cried I, and Thou heardest my voice."
[^447]: Daniel 3:49–50: the Angel of the Lord making the interior of the Babylonian furnace as it were a moist whistling wind.
[^448]: Matthew 5:5 / LXX Psalm 36:11: "Blessed are the meek: for they shall inherit the earth."
[^449]: Daniel 3:23–28: the Three Holy Youths (Ananias, Azarias, and Misael) preserved in the Chaldean furnace.
[^450]: Hebrews 4:12: the word of God living, powerful, and sharper than any two-edged sword.
[^451]: Matthew 3:12: the winnowing fan separating the wheat of Orthodox confession from the chaff of heresy.
[^452]: LXX Psalm 103:2–3 (MT Psalm 104:2–3) and Jeremiah 5:22.
[^453]: LXX Psalm 83:6–8 (MT Psalm 84:5–7): ascents in the heart, progressing from strength to strength, and 2 Corinthians 3:18: "from glory to glory."
[^454]: LXX Psalm 46:5 ("the excellency of Jacob whom He loved") and LXX Psalm 44:3 ("fairer than the children of men").
[^455]: Moses dividing the Red Sea with his staff (Exodus 14:16, 21–28) as a prophetic type of St. Eustathius dividing the deep waters of Scripture and refuting the Arian heretics.
[^456]: Genesis 11:1–9, the confounding of tongues at the Tower of Babel (the "Chalanaean tower", Genesis 10:10 / Calneh in Shinar).
[^457]: St. Eustathius, having been deposed by the Arian party around 330, was exiled to Philippi or Trajanopolis in Thrace (viewed as the West from Antioch), where he died in steadfast Orthodox confession.
[^458]: Luke 1:68–79: the Benedictus (Canticle of Zechariah).
[^459]: James 2:13: "Mercy boasts against judgment."
[^460]: Philippians 3:20: "For our conversation [citizenship] is in heaven."
[^461]: Luke 1:48: the Magnificat ("For behold, from henceforth all generations shall call me blessed").
[^462]: Luke 1:42: "Blessed art thou among women."
[^463]: LXX Psalm 103:15 (MT Psalm 104:15): "Wine that makes glad the heart of man."
[^464]: February 22: Commemoration of the Finding of the Relics of the Holy Martyrs in the quarter of Eugenius (near the Eugenian Gate of Constantinople) during the reign of Emperor Arcadius (c. 395–408).
[^465]: The solemn translation of the newly uncovered relics by the Patriarch of Constantinople into the cathedral church, accompanied by miraculous cures and healings.
[^466]: Slavonic: *...ба́съ прїе́млющихъ, я́кѡ вѣ́рныя бж҃їя оу҆го́дники.* The initial word completes *мольба́съ* (the supplications) or *потре́басъ* / *тре́басъ* (the petitions) from the bottom of fol. 194v (leaf p190).
[^467]: The 1761 Pochaiv print reads *мꙋ́нцы*, a typographical error for *мч҃нцы* (*мꙋ́ченицы*, martyrs).
[^468]: Refers to Patriarch Thomas I of Constantinople (607–610), during whose patriarchate the relics of the holy martyrs at the Eugenius gate were discovered and solemnly translated.
[^469]: The printed text reads *со хрⷭ҇то* with the final letter omitted at the column margin; restored to *со хрⷭ҇то́мъ* (with Christ).
[^470]: Alludes to the uncovering and translation of the relics by Patriarch Thomas I of Constantinople (607–610).
[^471]: Slavonic: *мꙋ́нцы*, a recurring typographical variant in the 1761 Pochaiv print for *мч҃нцы* (*мꙋ́ченицы*, martyrs).
[^472]: Refers to the annual liturgical commemoration of the translation of the martyrs' relics at the Eugenius gate in Constantinople.
[^473]: The Greek acrostic reads: Τὸν θεῖον ἐν ᾄσμασι μέλπω Πολύκαρπον. Θεοφάνους (I sing in hymns of the divine Polycarp. By Theophanes).
[^474]: Alludes to the ancient account in the *Martyrium Polycarpi* (§15), where the fire arched around the martyr's body like a ship's sail filled with wind, leaving him unharmed until an executioner was commanded to pierce him with a dagger.
[^475]: St. Polycarp was a direct disciple of St. John the Theologian (the beloved disciple).
[^476]: Alludes to the famous encounter recorded by St. Irenaeus (*Adv. Haer.* III.3.4), where Marcion met Polycarp and said, "Recognize us!" to which Polycarp replied, "I recognize thee, the first-born of Satan!" (here rendered *но́щнаго пе́рвенца*, the first-born of the night).
[^477]: Refers to the Gnostic dualism of Marcion and similar heretics, who taught two ultimate principles (a Demiurge or god of the Old Testament and the good God of the New Testament).
[^478]: A direct quotation from the *Martyrium Polycarpi* (§14: ὥσπερ κριὸς ἐπίσημος ἐκ μεγάλου ποιμνίου εἰς προσφοράν, ὁλοκαύτωμα δεκτὸν τῷ θεῷ ἡτοιμασμένον).
[^479]: St. Polycarp was a disciple of the holy Apostle and Evangelist John the Theologian (the disciple who spoke of God, *богословный ученикъ*).
[^480]: The First Finding of the honorable head of St. John the Baptist occurred in the fourth century on the Mount of Olives at the estate of Herod by two monks from the East; the Second Finding took place in the mid-fifth century (ca. 452) at Emesa (Homs in Syria) when the Baptist revealed the location of his head to Archimandrite Marcellus.
[^481]: According to tradition, the honorable head was placed in an earthen vessel (*въ сосудѣ скудельнѣ*) and hidden in the earth to prevent desecration.
[^482]: Slavonic: *пхли́ющыѧ* (an archaic or dialectal variant of *пха́ющыѧ*, those who shove, buffet, or oppress).
[^483]: Wisdom 4:7, 16–19; 5:1–7.
[^484]: Slavonic: *вѣ́честнаѧ ра́ка*, a typographical error in the 1761 Pochaiv print for *всече́стнаѧ ра́ка* (all-venerable shrine/reliquary).
[^485]: Cf. John 1:29.
[^486]: Luke 7:17–30.
[^487]: Slavonic: *жела́нїецъ тѧ̀ гра́дъ* (a corrupt or literal slavonization of the Greek Ἐμεσηνῶν πόλις, or reading *жела́нїемъ* as "the city that longed for thee", namely Emesa/Homs, where the head was discovered).
[^488]: Slavonic: *въ водоно́сѣ* (in a water-pot / hydria), alluding to the vessel in which the honorable head was concealed at Emesa.
[^489]: Slavonic: *вла́сяными рь́бы* (with raiment/garments of hair, cf. Matt 3:4).
[^490]: Cf. Matt 11:7: "What went ye out into the wilderness to see? A reed shaken with the wind?"
[^491]: Alludes to the condemnation of Arian or Macedonian heretics who denied the full divinity of the Son and Holy Spirit.
[^492]: Slavonic: *тѣ́мже да не оу҆досто́иши* (a typographical error in the 1761 Pochaiv print for *тѣ́мже да́же оу҆досто́иши* or *тѣ́мже ны́нѣ оу҆досто́иши*, "therefore do thou vouchsafe to be my helper").
[^493]: Slavonic: *запѣ́тѣвшымъ сердцы́амъ*, a typographical corruption for *оу҆питѣ́вшымъ сердцы́амъ* (unto hearts that have grown fat/callous, cf. Isa 6:10 / Matt 13:15: ἐπαχύνθη ἡ καρδία τοῦ λαοῦ τούτου).
[^494]: Refers once more to the city of Emesa where the honorable head was concealed and subsequently discovered.
[^495]: Alludes to the miracle associated with the discovery or translation of the relic, where a withered or deadened hand was healed or an unbeliever's hand was punished and restored upon venerating the holy head.
[^496]: The rubric in the printed Slavonic text reads 'Второ́е:' (Second), introducing an additional troparion to Ode 9 commemorating the gathering of all the righteous with the Forerunner.
[^497]: Printed Slavonic text reads 'Сечтнꙋ́ю' (a typographical error for 'Всечестнꙋ́ю' or 'Честнꙋ́ю').
[^498]: Printed Slavonic text reads 'ѡ҆чнще́нїе' with an erroneous 'н' for 'и'.
[^499]: Printed Slavonic text reads 'Вѣ́стина' (a typographical error for 'И҆́стина').
[^501]: Printed Slavonic text reads 'Сꙋдо́би' (a typographical error for 'Сподо́би').
[^500]: Printed Slavonic text reads 'чистны́ѧ' (a typographical error for 'честны́ѧ').
[^502]: In the printed Slavonic edition, the words 'Сла́ва ѻ҆це́въ' appear above the line, correcting the subsequent text to 'посе́мъ є҆кте́нїа' (thereafter the litany).
[^503]: Printed Slavonic text reads 'мꙋ́нченъ' (an abbreviated typographical form for 'мꙋ́чениченъ', the sticheron to the holy martyrs).
[^504]: Saint Tarasius, Patriarch of Constantinople, whose feast day is celebrated on February 25.
[^505]: The rubrics for Monday of the second week of the Great Fast are given above on fol. 209v (see physical leaf p220).
[^506]: In the Slavonic printed text, the incipit of "Let my prayer be set forth" (*Да исправится молитва моя*) is printed corruptly at the top of fol. 210v as "Зрѝ моѧ̀:", with a correction mark "Зрѝ" (*Behold*) printed in the left margin.
[^507]: Referring to the prescribed order for the Forerunner when celebrated on a weekday of the Great Fast without the Presanctified Liturgy, on fol. 210r (physical leaf p221).
[^508]: The Slavonic text misprints the incipit as *Покла́нїѧ ѿве́рзи мѝ две́ри* (using *Покла́нїѧ*, "worship/prostrations", in place of *Покаѧ́нїѧ*, "repentance").
[^509]: Incipit of the Katavasia of the Precious Cross, Ode 1 (*Боже́ственнѣйшїй проѡбразѝ* / *Θειότατος προετύπωσε*).
[^510]: The printed Slavonic text reads "консти́ннꙋ", a typographical corruption of "вои́стиннꙋ" (*truly* / Greek *ὡς ἀληθῶς*).
[^511]: The Pochaiv edition reads "коро́лю побѣ́дꙋ" (*victory for the king*), reflecting the Ruthenian liturgical adaptation for the reigning Polish-Lithuanian Crown (Augustus III), where Greek and Synodal Muscovite editions read "царю̀" / "царємъ" (*to the king/emperor* / *τοῖς βασιλεῦσι*).
[^512]: The Slavonic text reads "боѡбрази́вша" for "бо воѡбрази́вша" (*having formed [crosses]* / Greek *μορφώσαντας*).
[^513]: The printed Slavonic text reads "Размѣ́вше", an error for "Ра́зꙋмѣ́вше" (*having understood* / Greek συνέντες).
[^514]: The printed Slavonic text reads "под̾", an evident typographical error for "поя̀" (*singing*).
[^515]: The printed Slavonic text reads "безалѣ́тный", a typographical error for "безлѣ́тный" (*timeless* / *ageless* / Greek ἄχρονον).
[^516]: In the Theotokion after Ode 6, the compound phrase across the column break is printed "дѣ́йствителныя лѣ́чбы сн҃а твоегѡ̀ пола- / глю́щи стра́сти" (i.e. "полага́ющи" / *applying*).
[^517]: The printed Slavonic text reads "житокопребыва́нми", a typographical error for "жестокопребыва́нми" (*by harsh dwelling* / *harsh way of life*).
[^518]: The printed Slavonic text reads "Семꙋ́дреннаго", an error for "сꙋемꙋ́дреннаго" (*vain-minded*).
[^519]: The Church Slavonic *лво́вꙋ* refers to the iconoclast Emperor Leo (Leo III the Isaurian or Leo V the Armenian), whose persecutions Saint Basil the Confessor and Saint Prokopios manfully opposed.
[^520]: The physical Pochaiv 1761 text reads *И҆́гла оу҆́бѡ сꙋ́щихъ на землѝ* ("A needle indeed of those upon earth..."), likely a typographer's corruption or misreading of an earlier Slavonic witness (perhaps *И҆́го оу҆́бѡ*, "the yoke indeed", or reflecting Greek *ἄγλη* / *ἴλη*). Translated strictly as inked per MTS-1.
[^521]: Church Slavonic *ві́сиктъ* (from Greek *δίσεκτος* / Latin *bisextus*), denoting a leap year (bissextile year) containing 29 days in February.
[^522]: The physical Pochaiv 1761 print reads *Препобе́нъ бы́въ* (a typographer's error with *б* for *д*, i.e. *Преподо́бенъ бы́въ*, "Being made venerable"). Translated according to the evident emendation and documented per MTS-1.
[^523]: Church Slavonic *Оу҆хи́тилсѧ є҆сѝ* (from *ухитити* / *восхитити*, to snatch up or catch up, translating Greek *ἡρπάγης*).
[^524]: The physical leaf bears the printed signature *ркв* (fol. 222r), repeating the foliation numeral of leaf p245; it is sequentially folio 223r.
[^525]: Physical leaf p249 bears the printed folio numeral *д* with titlo (fol. 224r), continuing the single-digit or abbreviated foliation of the quire.
[^526]: Ps. 64:5 (LXX) [Ps. 65:4].
[^527]: The printed text shows the rubric *ве́чера* ('evening') printed in cinnabar ink directly above *ꙋ́тра* ('morning'), rectifying the weekly liturgical sequence.
[^528]: CS. *Ст҃опервочи́стаѧ* (*Святопервочистая*), a rare compound epithet ('holy and primordially pure').
[^529]: CS. *Зелмѝ* (*зѣлми*), archaic adverb meaning 'exceedingly, grievously'.
[^530]: CS. *Ѻ҆́ко срⷣца моегѡ̀ воспꙋща́ю къ тебѣ̀ Влⷣчце* ('The eye of my heart I direct unto thee, O Sovereign Lady').
[^531]: Cf. Luke 15:9.
[^532]: Rubric governing services when *Alleluia* replaces *God is the Lord* (e.g. during Great Lent or on days of fasting).
[^533]: Cf. Dan. 2:34, 45. CS. *неизглаго́ланнѡ* ('ineffably' / without human hands).
[^534]: Cf. Luke 1:38 and Genesis 18:9–15. The troparion contrasts the obedient faith of the Virgin Mary with Sarah's incredulous laughter in the tent when promised a son in her old age.
[^535]: Cf. Judges 6:36–40 (Gideon's fleece) and Psalm 71:6 [LXX 71:6 / MT 72:6]: "He shall come down like rain upon the fleece."
[^536]: The inner margin of fol. 231r exhibits ink abrasion and surface loss along the left edge; the bracketed text restores the standard Oktoechos Tone 7 Dismissal Theotokion formula.
[^537]: The outer margin of fol. 231v has slight trimming and paper rubbing along the right column; the bracketed text restores the universal Tone 8 Resurrectional Dismissal Theotokion formula.
[^538]: Autograph inscription of Metropolitan Andrey Sheptytsky (1865–1944), Ukrainian Greek Catholic Metropolitan of Halych and Archbishop of Lviv: (*Предложено в часі канонічної візитації*) (Presented during the canonical visitation), dated in Lviv on 18 March 1934 with his episcopal cross and signature (*+ Андрей М(итрополит)*).
[^539]: The spine preserves the 20th-century Ukrainian archival paper label: (*МІНЕЯ / ПОЧАЇВ 1761 / лютий 3 / Стар 171/3 (Інв 353)*) (with earlier inscription (*січень*) struck through), recording the volume's designation as February (Volume 3 of the winter quarter) and its institutional library inventory shelfmark.
